- F–E: connected by a continuous line.
- E–M: connected.
- M–J: connected.
- J–D: connected.

No shorter path exists because:
- Distance 1 and 2 frontiers did not include D.
- The first occurrence of D in BFS is at distance 4.

Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gpt-5
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 4
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
- Target black nodes: G and N.
- List neighbors and main corridors from G toward N:
  1) G → H → B → N
  2) G → H → C → N
  3) G → H → I → C → N
  4) G → J → C → N
  5) G → J → I → C → N
  6) G → J → P → O → N
  7) G → K → (H via edge K–H) → ... → N
  8) G → K → J → ... → N
  9) G → F → E → B → N
  10) G → F → M → O → N
  11) G → F → E → (to right via E–M–O) → N
  12) G → X → K → ... → N
- Thus, every route from G to N must eventually pass through one of the “gateway” nodes {H, J, F}. From these, paths continue to N via B/C/I/P/O or via E/M/O, all bypassing each other; hence removing only one of {H, J, F} leaves other gateways available.

Verification of cut {H, J, F}:
- Remove H: eliminates routes 1–3 and any via K→H.
- Remove J: eliminates routes 4–6 and K→J.
- Remove F: eliminates routes through E/M (9–11) and blocks entry into the top cluster; X routes go to K then to J or H, both already removed.
- With H, J, F removed, G has no remaining neighbor that can reach N: G’s remaining adjacent nodes are K and X; K connects only to H and J (both removed) and maybe to G; X connects to F and K (both unusable). Therefore, all paths are cut.

Minimality:
- Any 2-node subset fails: 
  - Remove H and J → path G→F→E/M→…→O→N remains.
  - Remove H and F → path G→J→…→N remains.
  - Remove J and F → path G→H→…→N remains.
Hence at least 3 nodes are required, and {H, J, F} is a valid minimum cut.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [H, J, F]

Image name: POGBa_task2_gt2_PAIR4
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gpt-5
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 4
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:

Target nodes: F (left-center) and J (top-right).

List all possible connections from F outward:
- F connects to X, K, G (via the small rectangle), and E (down).
Thus any path from F must go through one of {X, K, G, E}.

Check how these four neighbors lead toward J:
- Through X: X–G–H–I–P–J or X–K–(up-right loop via K–… none to J). The only progress to the right is via G → H → I → P → J.
- Through G: G–H–I–P–J.
- Through K: K connects only to X and the rectangle to F; it cannot reach right except via X→G→… So K’s route still funnels into G/H.
- Through E: E–B–C–I–P–J or E–B–…–O–P–J; also E–M–(outer frame)–…–J. However, all these routes to J must pass through either I or P before reaching J, because J’s only neighbor is P (plus the short loop back to P). Therefore, any route to J must reach P, and the only ways into P from the rest of the graph are via I or O or D. From F’s side, to reach O or D, paths still go through I or C→O or H→I→P, etc.

Key bottlenecks near J:
- J connects only to P.
- P connects to I, O, D, and J.
From the F side, reaching P necessarily goes through either I or O (D has no connection back left except via P, so it’s not reachable from F without first reaching P). Therefore, to block all paths to J it suffices to remove both I and O (cutting all entries to P reachable from F).

Verify minimality:
- Removing only I: a path remains F–X–G–H–I is blocked, but F–E–B–C–O–P–J or F–…–O–P–J still works via O.
- Removing only O: a path remains F–X/G–H–I–P–J via I.
- Removing I and O together: Every route from F toward J is forced to try to reach P, but P becomes unreachable since its only remaining neighbors are D and J; D is not reachable from F without going through P. Thus all F→J paths are broken.

No single node elsewhere (like H, C, B, E, G, X) suffices, because there exist alternate routes that bypass it via the other side (e.g., blocking H still allows E–B–C–I–P–J).

Therefore the minimum cut set size is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [I, O]

Image name: FD-K_task2_gt3_PAIR4
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gpt-5
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 4
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
- Target nodes are H and N.
- List connections near H: H connects to B, E, and I (via the slanted edge from H to I).
- List connections near N: N connects to B, C, and O, and to Y.
- Any H→N path must leave H through one of {B, E, I} and must enter N through one of {B, C, O} (Y only connects to N and O, so entry via Y implies coming via O first).

Enumerate path families:
1) Through B: H–B–N (direct), or H–B–C–N, H–B–C–O–N, etc.
2) Through E: H–E–…–(I/J/P/M/L or back to B/C/O)–…–(B/C/O)–N.
3) Through I: H–I–…–(J/P/C/O)–…–N.

To disconnect H from N, we must cut all three exits from H or all three entries to N.
- Cutting entries {B, C, O} would work but is size 3.
- Cutting exits {B, E, I} is also size 3.
Try mixed smaller cuts:

- If we remove B and C (size 2), path H–I–O–N remains (since I–O edge exists).
- If we remove B and O, path H–I–C–N remains.
- If we remove C and O, path H–B–N remains (direct through B).
- If we remove B and I, path H–E–…–(to C or O)–N exists (e.g., H–E–I–O–N via E–I edge).
- If we remove B and E, path H–I–O–N remains.
- If we remove E and I, path H–B–N remains.

Therefore any 2-node set leaves at least one route. Hence minimum is 3.

One valid minimal cut is removing the three neighbors of N: B, C, and O. Verification:
- With B removed, the direct H–B–N and any routes via B vanish.
- With C removed, routes H→(B/E/I)→…→C→N vanish.
- With O removed, routes entering via O (including via Y) vanish.
- No node remains adjacent to N except Y, which only connects to O; with O removed, N becomes isolated.
Thus H has no path to N.

No 2-node cut suffices as shown above.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, C, O]

1) Direct via H: G–H–I.
2) Through K side: G–K–H–I (since K–H edge exists).
3) Through K/X/F to J/I: G–K–X–F–(M or E)–J–I (since M–J edge exists; also E has no direct to J).
   More concretely: F–M–J–I, or F–M–…–P–I via J–P.

5) Through F to H? F does not connect to H directly.
6) Through K to J directly? No K–J edge not present.
Thus, every path from G to I must pass through either:
- Node H (for the short left route), or
- The apex chain leading to J (via F/X/M/E→M) and then to I (through J).

Observe the “right-side gateway” into I from that apex is J (since from the F/X/M part, the only descent toward I is through M–J–I; there is no edge from M/E/F/X/K directly to I or C or P).

Therefore, node separators:
- Removing H blocks all routes that use H (G–H–I and those via K–H–I).
- Removing J blocks all routes that come from the top/apex region (via F/X/M/E) into I.
After removing H and J, no alternative remains: there is no edge from G’s neighborhood (G, K, X, F, E, M) to C or P or I without passing J, and the left route via H is gone.

Can a single node suffice?
- Removing H alone leaves G–X–F–M–J–I (still connected).
- Removing J alone leaves G–H–I (still connected).
- Removing K, X, F, M, or E does not work because G–H–I remains; conversely removing H but not J leaves the apex route.
Hence minimum cut size is 2.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [H, J]

4) Through L or M: E–L–M–E triangle only links back to E and to J via M? There is an edge M–J. Yes: M connects to J; J connects to I. Thus there is a path E–M–J–I. To block this, remove J or M. But if we already remove P and B, does E–M–J–I still exist? Yes, it doesn’t use B or P. So we must also block the J route. Removing M would also work, but M is only used for that path; however there is also E–L–M–J–I and E–M–J–I, both rely on M or J. Removing J is cleaner since J also participates in P–J–I style paths (P–J edge exists). There is an edge P–J, giving paths E–P–J–I. With P removed, that specific one is gone, but J remains a direct neighbor of I via other E entries through M. So to fully disconnect, we need to cut the two direct bridges B and P and the remaining neighbor J.

Verify sufficiency:
- With {B, P, J} removed:
  - Direct E–B–I and E–P–I paths are gone.
  - Any path via F must go through P to reach I’s side; P is removed, so blocked.
  - Any path via L/M goes M–J–I; J is removed, so blocked.
  - Any path via B’s top cluster (C, H, N, O, Y) requires passing through B to reach from E’s side because E connects to that cluster only through B or P; both are removed. No alternative edges from E/L/M/F to C/H/N/O/Y exist.
Therefore E and I are disconnected.

Minimality:
- Removing only two nodes is insufficient:
  - If we omit B, path E–B–I exists.
  - If we omit P, path E–P–I exists.
  - If we omit J, path E–M–J–I exists via M (and L if needed).
Thus at least three nodes are needed, and {B, P, J} is a size-3 cut.

  - Through G: E–G–H–I.
  - Through M: E–M–J–I (since M–J edge exists).
  - Through L: E–L has no further edge except back to E, so no route via L.
  - Through B: E–B–C–O–P–I (since B–C, C–O, O–P, P–I) and also E–B–N–Y–O–P–I etc.
  - Also via G to upper cluster: E–G–F–K–J–I and E–G–X–K–J–I, E–G–F–X–K–J–I, etc.
  - Through G to lower: E–G–H–C–O–P–I.
- Observe bottlenecks near I’s side: Nodes adjacent to I are H, J, P, and C (via diagonal I–C), and also J via edge I–J.
- Any E→I path must enter I through one of {H, J, P, C}.
- Check if we can cut with fewer than 3 nodes:
  - If we remove two nodes, we’d need to cover all four ingress options. Because there exist vertex-disjoint branches reaching each: 
    - To H: E–G–H.
    - To J: E–M–J or E–G–…–K–J.
    - To P: E–B–C–O–P (avoids H and J).
    - To C: E–B–C or E–G–H–C (avoids J and P).
  - Any pair removal among {H, J, P, C} leaves at least one remaining neighbor reachable from E by a path that avoids the removed pair.
- Therefore we need at least 3 nodes. One minimal cover is removing three of I’s neighbors such that the remaining one is no longer reachable from E without using one of the removed nodes.
- Take cut set [J, P, H]:
  - Remaining neighbor C is still adjacent to I, but from E to C all routes go through either H, P, or J:
    - E–B–C uses none of H/J/P? It uses B only. But C remains connected to B and N/O/Y; does any path from E to C avoid H/J/P? Yes: E–B–C directly. However E–B edge exists, so this would still reach C. Hence we must include C instead of one of the others.
- Use cut set [J, P, C]:
  - Check remaining neighbor H. Paths from E to H go via G: E–G–H. This avoids J, P, C, so I would still be reachable via H–I. So we must include H instead of one of them.
- Use cut set [H, J, C]:
  - Remaining neighbor P. From E to P: paths E–B–C–O–P are blocked at C; E–M–J–P blocked at J; E–G–… to P via K–J or via C/O also blocked. No alternative from E to P remains.
  - From E to I via others are blocked since H, J, C removed.
- Verify minimality: Any 2-node set cannot block all four ingress neighbors as shown. Thus 3 is minimum.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [H, J, C]

- After removal, E has degree 0, hence no path can reach E from H or anywhere else. All possible H→… paths terminate before reaching E.

  2) N → C → I → H.
  3) N → C → J → H.
  4) N → P → I → H.
  5) N → P → J → H.
  6) N → O → P → I → H.

- Removing B alone blocks the left-side routes (N→B→H and Y→B→H), but paths remain through C/I/J/P.
- Removing either I or J blocks one branch, but the other still reaches H. Also, P routes feed into both I and J.

  - Remove B and I: Remaining possible connectors to H are via J only. Check N’s access to J: N → C → J or N → P → J or via O → P → J. But J connects to H directly, so H is still reachable. Thus removing I is insufficient if J remains.

- Verify sufficiency: Remove {B, I, J}.
  - Left routes via B are gone. Y cannot reach H without B.
  - Right routes: Any path from N via C, P, or O must pass through either I or J to enter H (there is no other edge into H such as from C or P directly to H). With both I and J removed, H has no remaining neighbor reachable from N’s side.
  - No alternative route through E, G, K, F, X, M, L: H connects to G and to I and J and B; after removing B, I, J, the only remaining neighbor is G. But N has no path to G that bypasses B/I/J: N must go through B→E→… or through C/P→J/I→…; both are blocked. Thus all paths are severed.
- Minimality:
  - Any cut must include at least one node blocking the B→H adjacency; since that edge is unique, it must remove B (cannot remove H). 
  - After removing B, H is still reachable via I or J from N; to block the right side completely, both I and J are necessary because there are independent routes from N to I and to J via C/P/O. Hence no 2-node cut exists.

   - G → H → B → … (then same options as above toward J/P → D)

No single edge removal can disconnect F and D because F has two disjoint exits (to E and G). Thus 2 is minimal.

3) Through I: E–B–I–J or E–P–I–J (since P–I exists).
4) Through D: E–P–D–J (since P–D exists).
5) Any routes via F/G/K/X ultimately reach J only through P, I, or M (via links …–F–P or …–I–J etc.), so blocking those three junctions suffices.

Thus every E–J path must pass through at least one of the three edges adjacent to J: (J, P), (J, I), or (J, M). Removing all three isolates J from E, while any two removed still leave a path via the remaining third.

Minimality check:
- If we remove only two among {(J, P), (J, I), (J, M)}, the third provides a surviving path from E: via E–P–J, or E–B–I–J, or E–M–J respectively.
- No single edge cut works because there are at least three edge-disjoint paths E–P–J, E–B–I–J, and E–M–J.

Therefore the minimum cut size is 3.

- X→K must go to F to exit, but F removed blocks it.
- X→G directly is blocked if G removed.
- X→F directly is blocked if F removed.

Is one node enough?
- If only F is removed, path X→G→E→B→C exists (or via H/I/P/O).
- If only G is removed, path X→F→E→B/N→C exists.
So need at least two nodes.

- All X’s neighbors then either removed (G) or dead-end (K remains but connects only to X and F; F removed). No route from X to the rest.
- Since target nodes cannot be removed, this guarantees no path to C.

No smaller cut set works (size 1 insufficient as shown). Hence minimum is 2.

2) J → M → … → O → Y (outer right edge).

- If we remove P, paths via P (including J–D–P and J–P) are cut.
- If we remove M, the long outer-right route from J to O/Y via M is cut.
- If we remove I, all the central/bottom routes via C, H, B, N, etc. are cut.

- Removing any single node among {P, M, I} leaves another route (e.g., remove P, still J→I→… or J→M→…).
- Removing any two of them still leaves the third as a J–Y path.

  2) L–M–F–X–K–(to J’s side through the long L–J edge). But this still ultimately uses the L–J edge to reach J’s side; there is no separate edge from K/G/H/E to J besides via I/C/P or the L–J edge.

D connects to P only; hence a path via D must use J–D then D–P then back to J through P, which still relies on J–D. To isolate J, we can cut the edges incident to J that connect to the rest: (J, L), (J, I), and (J, P). Removing these three leaves no path to J because:
- Direct edge (J, L) is gone.
- Any route via the vertical chain must use (J, I); removed.
- Any route via the right triangle through P/D must use (J, P) or (J, D); cutting (J, P) and leaving (J, D) alone is safe because D connects only to J and P; without (J, P), D cannot connect to the rest without passing through J, and (J, D) alone does not create a path from outside to J.

Minimality:
- Removing fewer than 3 edges leaves at least one J-incident connection: 
  - If (J, L) remains, L connects directly to J.
  - If (J, I) remains, left/bottom area reaches I then J.
  - If (J, P) remains, right area reaches P then J (and via D–P as well).
Hence at least three edges are necessary, and the set of the three incident edges listed suffices.

  2) Through F/K/X to G then H: E–F–G–H, E–F–X–G–H, E–F–X–K–G–H, E–F–K–G–H, E–K–G–H, E–X–G–H, etc. All use G just before H.

  - With G removed, we still have paths E–M–J–I–H and E–B–C–H (or E–N–C–H). These are vertex-disjoint in the region near H (I vs C), so a single additional node cannot block both simultaneously unless we remove H or E (not allowed). Therefore at least 3 nodes are needed.

Final Answer:

To disconnect completely, we must remove all ways to reach B from M; the only ways into B are through {N, C, E, F}. Therefore removing any two is not sufficient if the other two remain reachable from M. Hence we need to remove all but at most zero neighbors that are reachable. Since all four are reachable from M via vertex-disjoint portions of the graph, we must cut three of them to ensure B has no reachable neighbor from M.

Verification of a size-3 cut:
- Remove nodes {N, E, F}. Remaining neighbor of B is C.
- Are there M–C paths after removal? Yes, but we must check: with N, E, F removed, the only edge into B is C–B. If we also remove C, B becomes isolated from M. Alternatively, choose any three among {N, C, E, F} whose removal leaves B with no remaining reachable neighbor from M.
- Minimum: If only two of {N, C, E, F} are removed, at least one of the remaining neighbors (e.g., C or E) is still reachable from M, yielding a surviving path to B. Hence 2 is insufficient.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [C, E, N] (any three among {C, E, N, F} work).
